        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all four fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. The Base model confirms LoRA fine-tuning as the operative variable (GAR-ours: 0.01$\\to$${\\sim}$0.50). Convergence near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 is expected given the shared GAR loss and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02); scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:training-dynamics}). ThreadGate significantly outperforms LoRA-only (Wilcoxon $p$\\,=\\,0.004, $r$\\,=\\,0.454; GAR-ours 0.064 vs 0.037; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the residual gap remains large (7.8$\\times$). A second seed (123) confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. TF evaluation uses IID UltraChat and 50 held-out conversations; AR evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,50 held-out conversations (mean 3.6 user turns). All fine-tuned variants converge under TF ($\\leq$2\\,pp spread); the 8--17$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all configurations. $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$: learned gate mean and std across 28 layers (LoRA-only has no gates). GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{AR ($N$\\,=\\,50)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & 95\\% CI & $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Baseline}} \\\\\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.019 & {\\scriptsize[.014,\\,.024]} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Gated variants}} \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.064} & {\\scriptsize[.041,\\,.091]} & .93\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.030 & {\\scriptsize[.023,\\,.038]} & --- \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.49 & 0.01 & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.51} & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.033 & {\\scriptsize[.025,\\,.043]} & .96\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Ungated}} \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.037 & {\\scriptsize[.027,\\,.050]} & 1.00 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_training_dynamics.pdf}\n  \\caption{TF-AR distribution shift: fine-tuned variants converge to GAR$\\approx$0.50 under TF but collapse 7.8--15.5$\\times$ under AR generation (PCGrad, not shown, extends to 16.7$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). Log scale; dashed lines show gap magnitude.}\n  \\label{fig:training-dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under TF evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 (7--8\\% decay T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. AR GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}).}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. We test inference-time recovery by applying additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits during generation. An initial sweep ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}a) reveals sharp nonlinearity: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (0.062$\\to$0.680) with 35\\% PPL rise; at $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates (0.959) but PPL degrades to 4.22. Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, mean 3.6 turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirms the pattern: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 recovers GAR 8.7$\\times$ (0.064$\\to$0.556) with only 9\\% PPL cost, identifying a practical operating point. At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}).\n\nThis provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause: manually correcting attention restores GAR, circumventing the exposure bias barrier. The experiment is diagnostic, requiring goal-token identification.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering ($\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~$N$\\,=\\,5 (10-turn): sharp nonlinearity at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ GAR recovery. (b)~$N$\\,=\\,50 (mean 3.6 turns): $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 recovers GAR 8.7$\\times$ with 9\\% PPL cost. Dotted: unsteered TF baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

        "totalLines": 66

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation). Steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}).\n\n\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling and downstream effects.} Scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} increases AR GAR-ours from 0.064 to 0.347 (5.4$\\times$), compressing the 7.8$\\times$ gap to 1.44$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.44 vs.\\ 1.43); a second seed yields 0.418 (gap 1.20$\\times$). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353; $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot). The gating benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with compounding over turns. KV cache drift is a downstream consequence; instruction retention ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) shows content compliance rising +23.8\\,pp ($p$\\,=\\,0.077) with GAR correlation $r$\\,=\\,0.200 ($p$\\,=\\,0.007), while format constraints show no change (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).",

        "startLine": 1,

      }

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn attention degradation.}\nRecent work has established that LLMs systematically degrade across conversation turns. \\citet{laban2025lost} showed that model performance drops on tasks placed in later turns, even when the tasks are identical to earlier ones. \\citet{liu2026intent} traced a related failure to intent mismatch, where models progressively shift from the user's original intent as dialogue extends. MT-Bench-101 \\citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \\citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. Their work characterizes the phenomenon; ours attempts a systematic training-time intervention and, in doing so, uncovers why lightweight interventions fail to transfer from training to inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction stability.}\n\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Split-softmax restructures the attention mechanism itself and requires no retraining, but cannot address exposure bias in auxiliary losses that depend on training-time optimization. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}).\n\n\\paragraph{Residual gating and attention manipulation.}\nReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero} showed residual scaling shapes optimization; \\citet{gromov2024unreasonable} showed removing up to half a model's layers preserves capability, paralleling our finding that LoRA-only matches gating variants. StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle content; Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea} introduces role-aware episodic attention. PASTA \\citep{zhang2024tell} steers attention by reweighting heads; our $\\beta$-steering applies additive bias for diagnostic purposes. All these operate at inference time or within the attention mechanism; ThreadGate operates on the residual stream at training time.\n\n\\paragraph{Gated attention (qiu2025).}\nMost relevant to our architecture is Gated Attention \\citep{qiu2025gated}, which places a head-specific sigmoid gate after scaled dot-product attention, breaking the $W_V \\cdot W_O$ low-rank bottleneck with query-dependent sparsity. Trained at scale (15B MoE, 3.5T tokens), it achieves PPL $-$0.265, MMLU $+$2.03, and reduces first-token attention concentration from 46.7\\% to 4.8\\% with $<$2\\% overhead. The key distinction is granularity: their gate operates per-head within a single forward pass (turn-agnostic), while ThreadGate conditions on turn position to modulate per-layer residual contributions across conversation turns. Our results demonstrate that even with turn conditioning, training-time attention objectives face a fundamental exposure bias barrier that head-level gating does not address because it never targets an auxiliary attention distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-task gradient conflict.}\nGradNorm \\citep{chen2018gradnorm}, PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and Nash-MTL \\citep{navon2022nash} treat gradient conflict as a root cause. We repurpose PCGrad as a diagnostic: eliminating NTP-GAR directional conflict does not improve GAR convergence---gradient conflict is a symptom, not the cause.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure bias.}\nTeacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference \\citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled Sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing; subsequent work proposed sequence-level objectives \\citep{ranzato2016sequence}, adversarial regularization \\citep{lamb2016professor}, self-recovery analysis \\citep{he2019exposure}, and search-based training \\citep{leblond2018searnn}. This literature focuses on primary objectives in single-turn settings. Two gaps remain: no work examines how exposure bias affects \\emph{auxiliary} objectives targeting attention distributions, and no multi-turn transformer equivalent of these RNN-era mitigations exists. We fill both: auxiliary attention losses converge under teacher forcing but collapse 8--27$\\times$ under AR generation, and scheduled sampling transfers to multi-turn transformer attention objectives.",
        "numLines": 19,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 19
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/related_work.tex"
  },

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",

_source line 1166 · 2026-05-23T12:33:58.847Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] 用户指令——主图和可视化布局大改

**核心变化：**

1. **删除 Figure 1（PaperBanana method overview）**。不要了。

2. **Headline result 作为主图（新 Figure 1）**：选择最能体现 paper topic（exposure bias 摧毁辅助注意力损失）的结果图作为 Figure 1。候选：
   - Per-turn GAR decay 曲线（TF vs AR 的 dramatic collapse）
   - TF vs AR GAR bar chart（8-17× gap 一目了然）
   - 或组合图（上面是 collapse，下面是 steering recovery）
   选最有视觉冲击力的。Figure 1 必须在前 2 页出现。

3. **正文可视化图 4-5 张**：按与 paper topic 的相关性排序，不是按文中提到的顺序。最相关的放最前面。建议排序：
   - Fig 1: Headline result（GAR collapse）
   - Fig 2: β-steering recovery（建设性证据，证明机制是注意力路由）
   - Fig 3: Gate shift analysis（诊断证据）
   - Fig 4: PCGrad 或 training dynamics
   - 如果空间允许再加一张

4. **美观度要高**：检查所有图的配色一致性、字号可读性、legend 清晰度。如果有图的质量不够高就不放主文（移到附录）。

5. **附录不要和正文重复**：检查附录中是否有和主文完全相同的图/表/数据。如果有，附录中改为补充细节（如更多层/更多条件/完整数字表），而非重复相同的可视化。

页面预算：主文严格 ≤8 页。删掉 Fig 1 会空出空间，但新增可视化图可能需要压缩文字。这次不用验证引用。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01893L268xqz9TcVswvhxKSX`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Fine-tuned to allocate 50\% of attention to system-prompt goals (GAR\,=\,0.50 under teacher forcing), Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct retains only GAR\,=\,0.064 under autoregressive generation---an 8$\times$ collapse. We trace this failure to exposure bias: attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text. ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates trained with an auxiliary GAR loss, provides a controlled testbed. A five-way comparison isolates turn conditioning, gating, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning; each pair kills one hypothesis. Gating outperforms LoRA-only ($p$\,=\,0.004) but the gap remains 7.8$\times$; PCGrad eliminates gradient conflict but \emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\,=\,0.002), ruling out the most intuitive failure mechanism by \emph{modus tollens}. Steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727; scheduled sampling compresses the gap to 1.44$\times$ (single seed). Cross-model evaluation on Llama-3.1-8B confirms a 27$\times$ gap. These findings establish that exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention losses while NTP self-recovers---a failure mode without prior multi-turn transformer characterization.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EfupCu2JL948kpCenpPAF9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	The five-way comparison (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \S\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.
4	
5	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
6	
7	The design intent of ThreadGate is to modulate the residual stream more aggressively at late conversation turns, where GAR naturally decays (\S\ref{sec:background}). We test whether the learned gates achieve this by comparing gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers of the trained model.
8	
9	The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$). For example, layer~23 gates from 0.769 (turn~1) to 0.979 (turn~21). Gates suppress more at early turns (high GAR) and become passive at late turns (low GAR), because NTP pressure---strongest at late turns with long contexts---pushes gates toward 1.0 to preserve sublayer computation.
10	
11	Inference-time gate values range from 0.93 to 0.98 (2--7\% attenuation), explaining the near-convergence in Table~\ref{tab:four-way}. Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}(a) visualizes the inversion (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior? If so, resolving it should rescue gate conditioning.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	  \centering
15	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth,height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
16	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base) under our evaluation conditions.}
17	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
21	
22	NTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\% of layers (mean cosine similarity $-$0.089 to $-$0.042), with NTP magnitude dominating 7.5--14.7$\times$. If this directional conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. We apply PCGrad to gate parameters only; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium violations \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
23	
24	PCGrad shifts cosine similarity from consistently negative to oscillating around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged (GAR-ours 0.50, PPL 2.78). AR GAR, however, \emph{worsens}: 0.064$\to$0.030 (Wilcoxon $p$\,=\,0.002; Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \emph{modus tollens}: if direction conflict causes the collapse, removing it should prevent it; PCGrad removes it; the collapse worsens; therefore direction conflict is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\times$ NTP magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential. With gradient conflict excluded, the failure must occur at the training-to-inference transition.
25	
26	\begin{figure}[t]
27	  \centering
28	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth,height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}
29	  \caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}
30	  \label{fig:training-pcgrad}
31	\end{figure}
32	
33	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
34	
35	The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: under standard training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property of the prefix with no autoregressive counterpart. TF GAR-ours remains near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 (7--8\% decay; Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}), yet AR GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).
36	
37	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
38	
39	We test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\times$) vs.\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.
40	
41	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
42	
43	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\,$>$\,100\%), so we report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$. Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\times$ and 17$\times$ (outlier spikes inflate means; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\times$ on Qwen, 27$\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias.
```

      "content": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nThe design intent of ThreadGate is to modulate the residual stream more aggressively at late conversation turns, where GAR naturally decays (\\S\\ref{sec:background}). We test whether the learned gates achieve this by comparing gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers of the trained model.\n\nThe gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$). For example, layer~23 gates from 0.769 (turn~1) to 0.979 (turn~21). Gates suppress more at early turns (high GAR) and become passive at late turns (low GAR), because NTP pressure---strongest at late turns with long contexts---pushes gates toward 1.0 to preserve sublayer computation.\n\nInference-time gate values range from 0.93 to 0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation), explaining the near-convergence in Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}. Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}(a) visualizes the inversion (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior? If so, resolving it should rescue gate conditioning.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base) under our evaluation conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (mean cosine similarity $-$0.089 to $-$0.042), with NTP magnitude dominating 7.5--14.7$\\times$. If this directional conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. We apply PCGrad to gate parameters only; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium violations \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity from consistently negative to oscillating around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged (GAR-ours 0.50, PPL 2.78). AR GAR, however, \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 (Wilcoxon $p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: if direction conflict causes the collapse, removing it should prevent it; PCGrad removes it; the collapse worsens; therefore direction conflict is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ NTP magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential. With gradient conflict excluded, the failure must occur at the training-to-inference transition.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: under standard training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property of the prefix with no autoregressive counterpart. TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 (7--8\\% decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}), yet AR GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%), so we report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$. Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$ (outlier spikes inflate means; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias.",
      "numLines": 43,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 43
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	\subsection{Preliminaries}\label{sec:background}
4	
5	\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}
6	We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \emph{goal tokens} $\mathcal{G}$. \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\tau$ as
7	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar}
8	  \text{GAR}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H \cdot |\mathcal{R}_\tau|} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{R}_\tau} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)},
9	\end{equation}
10	where $\mathcal{R}_\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\ell$, head $h$.
11	
12	\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}
13	\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. \textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement to the \emph{last token} of each assistant turn:
14	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar-ours}
15	  \text{GAR\text{-}ours}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{t_\tau,\, j}^{(\ell,h)},
16	\end{equation}
17	where $t_\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}).
18	
19	\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}
20	
21	ThreadGate adds a learned gating mechanism to the transformer's residual stream. At each layer $l$, the residual update is modulated by a scalar gate:
22	\begin{equation}
23	  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l),
24	\end{equation}
25	where $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or FFN). This resembles ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \emph{content-conditioned}---a function of the hidden state, not a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \emph{turn-conditioned}---incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\sigma(1.5) \approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.
26	
27	\paragraph{Gate network.} Each gate $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer MLP:
28	\begin{align}\label{eq:gate}
29	  g_l = \sigma\bigl(W_2\,\text{ReLU}(&W_1\,[\text{proj}(x_l);\;\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \notag\\
30	  &+ b_1) + b_2\bigr),
31	\end{align}
32	where $\text{proj}(\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\!=\!28$), this adds ${\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\% of 7B).
33	
34	\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\text{emb}(\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness.
35	
36	\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\sim}$82\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\times$ learning rate multiplier.
37	
38	\subsection{Training Objective}\label{sec:loss}
39	
40	The training objective combines three terms:
41	\begin{equation}
42	  \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{div}.
43	\end{equation}
44	$\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. The GAR loss targets a fixed attention level to goal tokens:
45	\begin{equation}
46	  \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} = \text{MSE}(\text{GAR\text{-}ours}, \tau),
47	\end{equation}
48	where $\tau = 0.5$. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \S\ref{sec:background}). The gate diversity loss prevents collapse to a uniform value:
49	\begin{equation}
50	  \mathcal{L}_\text{div} = \text{ReLU}(\sigma_\text{min} - \text{std}(\mathbf{g})),
51	\end{equation}
52	where $\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \ldots, g_L]$ and $\sigma_\text{min}$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$.
53	
54	\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure---penalizing both over- and under-attention---preventing identity collapse.
55	
56	\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\label{sec:comparison-design}
57	
58	We design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ Uniform Gate} (turn conditioning), \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ +PCGrad} (gradient direction conflict via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).
59	
```
